Vorticity blow-up for the 3D incompressible Euler equations
Wenjie Deng, Song Jiang, Minling Li, Zhaonan Luo

Abstract
In this paper, we study the finite-time blow-up for classical solutions of the 3D incompressible Euler equations with low-regularity initial vorticity. Applying the self-similar method and stability analysis of the self-similar system in critical Sobolev space, we prove that the vorticity of the axi-symmetric 3D Euler equations develops a finite-time singularity with certain scaling indices. Furthermore, we investigate the time integrability of the solutions. The proof is based on the new observations for the null structure of the transport term, and the parameter stability of the fundamental self-similar models.



1
Introduction

In this paper, we intend to investigate the Euler equations governing ideal fluid flow as:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{u}+\bm{u}\cdot\nabla\bm{u}+\nabla P=0,,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle{\rm div}~\bm{u}=0,\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\bm{u}|_{t=0}=\bm{u}_{0},\end{array}\right.

where
\bm{u}
denotes the velocity field of the fluid, and
P
represents the force of internal pressure. A central quantity in fluid dynamics is the vorticity field
\bm{\omega}\triangleq\nabla\times\bm{u}
, which evolves according to

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{\omega}+\bm{u}\cdot\nabla\bm{\omega}=\bm{\omega}\cdot\nabla\bm{u},~~~~{\rm div}~\bm{\omega}=0,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3},\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\bm{\omega}|_{t=0}=\bm{\omega}_{0}=\nabla\times\bm{u}_{0},\end{array}\right.

The velocity field can be reconstructed from vorticity via the Biot-Savart law:

\displaystyle\bm{u}=(-\Delta)^{-1}(\nabla\times\bm{\omega}).

(1.6)

Research on the Euler equations (
1
) has advanced significantly in the past century, profoundly deepening our understanding of inviscid flow.
Relevant references are summarized below to further elucidate the behavior of inviscid fluids. The early foundational work by Lichtenstein
[
11
]
and Gunther
[
7
]
established local well-posedness in Hölder spaces with suitable decay. The two-dimensional case saw significant advances with Yudovich
[
14
]
, who proved the existence and uniqueness of global weak solutions. For the three-dimensional case, a major breakthrough came with the work of Beale et al.
[
1
]
, which provided the BKM criterion for determining the global existence of strong solutions to the Euler equations.
To be specific, a classical solution for equations (
1
)-(
1.6
) cannot be continued in class
\{\bm{u}|\bm{u}\in C([0,T);H^{s}(\mathbb{R}^{3}))\cap C^{1}([0,T);H^{s-1}(\mathbb{R}^{3})),s\geq 3\}
as
t
goes to
T
if and only if

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow T}\int_{0}^{t}\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\bm{\omega}(t,\bm{x})|ds=+\infty.

Further contributions include the double exponential growth result for 2D flows by Kiselev and Šverák
[
10
]
, local well-posedness with critical regularity by Guo et al.
[
8
]
, ill-posedness in borderline Sobolev spaces by Bourgain and Li
[
2
]
, and the resolution of Onsager’s conjecture by Isett
[
9
]
and Buckmaster et al.
[
3
]
. Among many other notable contributions not covered in detail here, these works have collectively advanced our mathematical understanding of ideal fluids.

However, a fundamental gap persists between current mathematical theory and the understanding required to fully characterize solutions of the Euler equations, prompting the following global regularity problem:

Question:

Let
\bm{u}\in C^{\infty}([0,T)\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
be a smooth solution to the Euler equations (
1
). Can the derivative of the velocity field blow up in finite time
T
? That is, it is possible that

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow T}\sup_{\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}}|\nabla\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})|=+\infty~?

Although this question for smooth solutions remains open, a significant breakthrough was recently achieved by Elgindi
[
5
]
, who established finite-time blow-up for a class of low-regularity solutions to the Euler equations. More precisely, Elgindi constructed self-similar solutions with a finite-time singularity, which satisfy certain symmetries at regularity
C^{1,\alpha}
with
\alpha>0
small. Subsequently, Elgindi et al.
[
6
]
used a truncation argument to show that these self-similar solutions are stable and persist in the energy space
H^{1}
up to the blow-up time.
More recently, instead of using self-similar coordinates, Córdoba et al.
[
4
]
introduced a novel mechanism to constructe finite-time singularities for a class of low-regularity solutions that remain smooth away from a single point. Nevertheless, the details until the blow-up time are still poorly understood. For example, it is unclear what quantities remain bounded as opposed to those that diverge, what structure such as point vortices forms, and whether the solution can be continued in a weak sense. A rigorous understanding of these issues is essential both mathematically and physically.

In an effort to understand these questions, in this paper a refined framework is developed to construct finite-time singularities with a prescribed structure and characterize the extent of integrability of the resulting solutions at the blow-up time. More precisely, a scaling-related parameter
\beta
is introduced, and its effect on the regularity and integrability of solutions is systematically analyzed. The value of
\beta
is shown to play a critical role in governing the asymptotic behavior of the solution. In particular, a suitable choice of
\beta
produces solutions with the specific desired integrability properties. These provide a foundation for systematically constructing and analyzing solutions with finite-time singularities within more specific function spaces.

1.1.  Statement of main theorem

Firstly, we give the definition of the odd function as follows:

Definition 1.1
.

A velocity vector field
\bm{u}=(u_{1},u_{2},u_{3}):\mathbb{R}^{3}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{3}
is called
odd
if
u_{i}
is odd in
x_{i}
and even in the other two variables for any
i=1,2,3
.

From now on, we only consider the odd solution
\bm{u}
of the Euler equations (
1
).
We define the two-dimensional radial variable
r\triangleq\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}}
. Then the three-dimensional radial variable is given by
|\bm{x}|\triangleq\sqrt{r^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}
, which expands to
\sqrt{x_{1}^{2}+x_{2}^{2}+x_{3}^{2}}
.
The vorticity components in the
r
and
x_{3}
directions vanish identically, while the
\widetilde{\theta}
component (i.e., the angle between
x_{1}
and
x_{2}
axes) is denoted by
\omega
.
To simplify the analysis, we impose an odd symmetry on
\omega
with respect to the
x_{3}
-axis.
Specifically, we seek solutions satisfying the following condition:

\displaystyle\omega(t,r,x_{3})=-\omega(t,r,-x_{3}).

The axi-symmetric 3D Euler equations with vanishing swirl can be expressed as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\omega+u_{r}\partial_{r}\omega+u_{3}\partial_{3}\omega=\frac{u_{r}}{r}\omega,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~(r,x_{3})\in D_{0},\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle-\partial^{2}_{r}(\psi)-\partial^{2}_{3}(\psi)-\frac{\partial_{r}\psi}{r}+\frac{\psi}{r^{2}}=\omega,\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle u_{r}=\partial_{3}\psi,~~u_{3}=-\frac{\psi}{r}-\partial_{r}\psi,\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\omega|_{t=0}=\omega_{0},\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\omega|_{\partial D_{0}}=0,~~\psi|_{\partial D_{0}}=0,\end{array}\right.

where the domain
D_{0}\triangleq[0,\infty)\times[0,\infty)
.
We introduce some parameters, which will be commonly used in this paper, as follows:

\displaystyle\alpha>0,~~~~\beta\in(0,1],~~~~\eta\in(0,1),~~~~\lambda=1+\frac{\alpha}{10\beta}>1.

(1.12)

Then, we define the backward self-similar solution with the scaling index
\beta_{0}
as follows:

Definition 1.2
.

The function
\omega(t,x)
is the so-called backward self-similar solution, if it takes the form

\displaystyle\omega(t,x)=\frac{1}{T_{0}-t}\widetilde{\Omega}\left(\frac{x}{(T_{0}-t)^{\beta_{0}}}\right),

for some positive constants
T_{0}
and
\beta_{0}
. We then assert that
\omega(t,x)
develops a finite-time singularity at
t=T_{0}
with the scaling index
\beta_{0}
.

Through a combination of self-similar analysis, nonlinear stability and energy estimates, we prove that classical solutions to the 3D Euler equations (
1.1
) exhibit the finite-time vorticity singularity in this paper.
Now, we can state our main result.

Theorem 1.1
.

For any
\beta\in(0,1]
, there exists a positive constant
\alpha(\beta)
, the initial vorticity
\omega_{0}\in C^{0,\frac{\alpha}{20\beta}}(D_{0})
with
|\omega_{0}|\leq c|\bm{x}|^{\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}(1+|\bm{x}|)^{-\left({\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}+\frac{\alpha}{2}}\right)}
for some constant
c>0
, and the positive time
T^{\ast}
, such that the axi-symmetric 3D Euler equations (
1.1
) admit a solution

\omega\in C^{0,\frac{\alpha}{2\beta}}_{t}([0,T^{\ast});C^{0,\frac{\alpha}{20\beta}}_{x}(D_{0}))
.
The solution develops a finite-time singularity at
t=T^{\ast}>0
with scaling index
\frac{\beta}{\alpha}
.
Moreover, there exists a parameter
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
that satisfies
|\frac{\beta}{\gamma}-1|\ll 1
and
T^{\ast}=\frac{1}{2}+\frac{\beta}{2\gamma}
, such that the following blow-up results hold:

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow T^{\ast}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\omega(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{0})}ds=+\infty,\quad\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow T^{\ast}}\int_{0}^{t}\Big\|\frac{u_{r}}{r}(s)\Big\|_{L^{\infty}(D_{0})}ds=+\infty.

(1.13)

Furthermore, for any
1\leq p<\frac{2}{2-\beta}
, the velocity components exhibit the following integrability property:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T^{\ast}}\|(u_{r},u_{3})(s)\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}_{loc}(D_{0})}ds<+\infty.

(1.14)

Remark 1.2
.

In Theorem
1.1
, we investigate the time integrability of the solutions at the blow-up time in relation to the parameter
\beta
.
The special case
\beta=1
yields the space-time integrability result
(u_{r},u_{3})\in L^{p}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}_{loc}(D_{0}))
for any
p\in[1,2)
.
We note that this result can, in fact, be extended to a broader range of
p
.
The restriction to
\beta\in(0,1]
in our current formulation arises from the requirement that all terms in the
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
-norm definition (see (
5.5
) in Section
5
) remain positive.
Nevertheless, the term
\frac{1-\beta}{2c_{\ast,\beta}\beta}\|L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)w_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{z}}
can be controlled by
\frac{2-\beta}{2}\|fw^{K}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}
via Lemma
5.3
in Section
5
.
Thus, there exists a constant
\delta>0
, such that Theorem
1.1
holds for
\beta\in(0,1+\delta]
.
Consequently, for
\beta=1+\delta
, we can obtain the space-time integrability result that
(u_{r},u_{3})\in L^{2}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}_{loc}(D_{0}))
.

It is straightforward to verify that the Euler equations (
1
)-(
1.6
) admit the solutions that develop the finite-time singularity, as an immediate corollary of Theorem
1.1
.

Corollary 1.3
.

For any
\beta\in(0,1]
, there exists a positive constant
\alpha_{0}(\beta)
, a divergence-free and odd initial velocity
\bm{u}_{0}
, the corresponding vorticity
\bm{\omega}_{0}\in C^{0,\alpha_{0}}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
with a positive constant
\alpha_{0}
, and the positive time
T^{\ast}
, such that the 3D Euler equations (
1
)-(
1.6
) admit a solution
\bm{\omega}\in C^{0,\alpha_{0}}([0,T^{\ast})\times\mathbb{R}^{3})
.
Moreover, the following blow-up result holds:

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow T^{\ast}}\int_{0}^{t}\|\bm{\omega}(s)\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds=+\infty.

Furthermore, for any
1\leq p<\frac{2}{2-\beta}
, the velocity exhibits the following integrability property:

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T^{\ast}}\|\bm{u}(s)\|^{p}_{L^{\infty}_{loc}(\mathbb{R}^{3})}ds<+\infty.

Remark 1.4
.

It should be noted that
\alpha(\beta)
in Theorem
1.1
and
\alpha_{0}(\beta)
in Corollary
1.3
, while not identical, are both small parameters.

1.2.  Reformulation

We now proceed to reformulate equations (
1.1
) in polar coordinates.
For this purpose, we define:

•

The three-dimensional radial variable with index
\alpha
as
R\triangleq(r^{2}+x_{3}^{2})^{\frac{\alpha}{2}}
with
\alpha>0
;

•

The angle variable as
\theta\triangleq\arctan(\frac{x_{3}}{r})
, representing the angle between
r
and
x_{3}
.

We also define the differential operator
D_{R}\triangleq R\partial_{R}
.
We introduce the new unknown functions as:

\Omega(t,R,\theta)\triangleq\omega(t,r,x_{3}),\quad R^{\frac{2}{\alpha}}\Psi(t,R,\theta)\triangleq\psi(t,r,x_{3}),

then the evolution equation for
\Omega
can be written as:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\Omega+U(\Psi)\partial_{\theta}\Omega+V(\Psi)\alpha D_{R}\Omega=\mathcal{R}(\Psi)\Omega,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~(R,\theta)\in D,\\[4.30554pt]
L^{\alpha}_{R}(\Psi)+L_{\theta}(\Psi)=\Omega,\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega|_{\partial D}=0,~~\Psi|_{\partial D}=0,\end{array}\right.

where the domain
D\triangleq[0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
,
U(\Psi)
,
V(\Psi)
and
\mathcal{R}(\Psi)
are defined by

U(\Psi)\triangleq-3\Psi-\alpha D_{R}\Psi,~~V(\Psi)\triangleq\partial_{\theta}\Psi-(\tan\theta)\Psi,~~\mathcal{R}(\Psi)\triangleq V(\Psi)-(\tan\theta)U(\Psi).

Moreover, the radial operator
L^{\alpha}_{R}
and the angular operator
L_{\theta}
are defined by

L^{\alpha}_{R}(\Psi)\triangleq-\alpha^{2}D^{2}_{R}\Psi-5\alpha D_{R}\Psi,~~L_{\theta}(\Psi)\triangleq-\partial_{\theta}V(\Psi)-6\Psi.

Next, we introduce two functions that depend solely on angular
\theta
as:

\displaystyle K(\theta)\triangleq(\sin\theta)(\cos\theta)^{2},

(1.18)

and for any
a>0
,

\Gamma_{\theta,a}(\theta)\triangleq(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3a}}.

The function spaces
\mathcal{H}^{k}
and
\mathcal{E}^{2}
will be defined in Section
2
.
Considering the system (
1.2
), we have the following theorem.

Theorem 1.5
.

For any
\beta\in(0,1]
, there exists a positive constant
\alpha(\beta)
, such that the system (
1.2
) admits a solution
\Omega
that develops a finite-time singularity.
Moreover, there exists a function
g\in\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
and a parameter
\gamma\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
satisfying
|\frac{\beta}{\gamma}-1|\ll 1
, such that

\Omega(t,R,\theta)=\frac{1}{t_{\gamma}}F\left(\frac{R}{t_{\gamma}^{\beta}},\theta\right),~~~F(z,\theta)=F^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z,\theta)+g(z,\theta),~~~t_{\gamma}=1-\frac{2\gamma}{\gamma+\beta}t,~~~z=\frac{R}{t_{\gamma}^{\beta}},

where
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
is defined as

F^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z,\theta)\triangleq\frac{4\alpha}{3\gamma}\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)}{\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}K(\theta)\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)d\theta}\frac{z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{2}}.

Remark 1.6
.

Let
\gamma=1
. Elgindi proved that there exists a positive constant
\alpha
, such that the system (
1.2
) admits solutions
\Omega
that develop singularity in finite time, see
[
5
]
. Specifically, there exists a function
g\in\mathcal{H}^{4}
with the different parameter
\lambda=1+\frac{\alpha}{10}
, and a parameter
\beta\in\mathbb{R}^{+}
with
|\beta-1|\ll 1
, such that

\Omega(t,R,\theta)=\frac{1}{t_{1}}F\left(\frac{R}{t_{1}^{\beta}},\theta\right),~~~F(z,\theta)=F_{1}^{\ast}(z,\theta)+g(z,\theta),~~~t_{1}=1-\frac{2}{\beta+1}t,~~~z=\frac{R}{t_{1}^{\beta}}.

He established that
F^{\ast}_{1}
, which is smooth in
z
, lies in
\mathcal{W}^{l,\infty}
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.

Remark 1.7
.

The fundamental self-similar solutions
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, constructed in Theorem
1.5
, is well-defined for all
\gamma>0
.
It should be noted when
\frac{1}{\gamma}\not\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
,
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
fails to be smooth as
z
goes to
0
.
We further prove that
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
, where the space
\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
is introduced in Section
2
.
Additionally, the parameter stability of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
is established in Section
3
.

Remark 1.8
.

Here, we highlight the critical issue mentioned in Section
8
of
[
5
]
:
“In
\mathcal{H}^{2}
the problem becomes critical in a certain sense, and it is not clear whether the non-linear estimates can be closed in an easy way.”
Through a rigorous analysis in Section
7
, we introduce a new second-order space
\mathcal{E}^{2}
equipped with a stronger singular weight and establish a priori energy estimates in
\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
by exploiting the null structure of the transport term.
Finally, we establish the existence of the self-similar solution
g(z,\theta)
, see Theorem
1.5
for more details.
Moreover, we point out that
\mathcal{H}^{k}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
for any
k\geq 3
.

1.3.  Motivation and main ideas

The classical Navier–Stokes and Euler equations both exhibit scaling invariance, implying that their solutions possess no intrinsic length or time scales. And blow-up occurs as the flow undergoes intense amplification at vanishingly small spatial scales within an extremely short time interval. This process can naturally be described through scaling transformations.

This invariance leads to self-similar solutions, which retain their form under scaling up to the blow-up time
T
.
Substituting such an ansatz into the Euler equations reduces the original partial differential equation to a nonlinear eigenvalue problem for the profile
U(\xi)
, typically governed by an ordinary or simpler partial differential equation. The existence of a nontrivial profile
U(\xi)
implies the construction of a solution that develops a finite-time singularity at time
T
.

This paper leverages the very method outlined above to establish the existence of finite-time singularity solutions for the Euler equations (
1
)-(
1.6
) within a refined framework. Specifically, we construct a family of axisymmetric self-similar solutions with prescribed scaling index for the Euler equations (
1
)-(
1.6
).
Our approach consists of the following two steps:

Step (i):
First, for any
\gamma>0
, we construct a fundamental solution
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
and establish its stability with respect to the variations of the parameter
\gamma
. This foundational step enables the construction of a family of approximate solutions parameterized by
\gamma
.

Step (ii):
Next, for any given
0<\beta\leq 1
, we identify a fundamental solution indexed by
\gamma(\beta)
that approximates the solutions
F
to the self-similar form of system (
1.2
). This is achieved by deriving energy estimates of the perturbation solution
g\triangleq F-F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
in space
\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
with critical regularity.

Based on this analysis, the finite-time blow-up is established for both vorticity
\omega
and quantity
\frac{u_{r}}{r}
in the
L^{1}([0,t];L^{\infty}(D_{0}))
-norm as
t
approaches a finite time
T^{\ast}
(see Theorem
1.1
). Furthermore, the time integrability of these solutions is analyzed.

1.3.1 Motivation

We now proceed to a detailed analysis that originated from a conceptual exploration of scaling invariance in the Navier–Stokes equations:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\partial_{t}\bm{u}+\bm{u}\cdot\nabla\bm{u}+\nabla P-\nu\Delta\bm{u}=0,~~~~{\rm div}~\bm{u}=0,\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~\bm{x}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.\\[4.30554pt]
\displaystyle\bm{u}|_{t=0}=\bm{u}_{0}.\end{array}\right.

If
\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})
is a solution, then the only scaling preservation solutions for arbitrary
T_{0}>0
are

\bm{u}_{T_{0}}(t,\bm{x})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{T_{0}}}\bm{u}\left(\frac{t}{T_{0}},\frac{\bm{x}}{\sqrt{T_{0}}}\right).

This specific relation, where the spatial and temporal scaling exponents are coupled, reflects the constraint on scaling invariance due to viscosity.

Setting
\nu=0
reduces the Navier–Stokes equations to the Euler equations (
1
).
In this case, if
\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})
is a solution of the Euler equations, then for any scaling parameters
T_{0}>0
and
X_{0}>0
, the rescaled function

\bm{u}_{X_{0},T_{0}}(t,\bm{x})=\frac{X_{0}}{T_{0}}\bm{u}\left(\frac{t}{T_{0}},\frac{\bm{x}}{X_{0}}\right)

also satisfies the Euler equations (
1
), showing greater freedom in scaling invariance for inviscid flow.

Both the Navier–Stokes and Euler equations, for arbitrary
T_{0}>0
, admit a backward self-similar ansatz of the form

\displaystyle\bm{u}(t,\bm{x})=\frac{1}{(T_{0}-t)^{1-\beta_{0}}}\bm{U}\left(\frac{\bm{x}}{(T_{0}-t)^{\beta_{0}}}\right).

(1.19)

However, the different freedoms in scaling invariance lead to a fundamental distinction: the Euler equations allow
\beta_{0}
to take any positive real value, reflecting their unconstrained scaling invariance, while the Navier–Stokes equations, due to viscosity, require
\beta_{0}=\frac{1}{2}
.

This constraint has direct consequences for the existence of such solutions. Nečas, Ružička and Šverák
[
12
]
proved that no non-trivial backward self-similar solutions exists in the space
L^{3}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
for the Navier–Stokes equations, a result later extended by Tsai
[
13
]
to more general function spaces. These findings reveal a fundamental tension between integrability and the formation of self-similar singularities under scaling invariance, and identify the parameter
\beta_{0}
as a key indicator of the intrinsic scaling behavior of each equation.

These considerations motivate the following fundamental inquiry:

•

How can the parameter
\beta_{0}
be utilized to further characterize self-similar singularities?

To this end, this paper undertakes a refined quantification of
\beta_{0}
and systematically examines its influence on the system under study, thereby outlining the main ideas and proof methodology.

1.3.2 Main ideas

Our focus is on the backward self-similar solution of system (
1.2
). For any given
\beta\in(0,1]
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
that would be determined later, we consider the following self-similar variables:

\displaystyle t_{\mu}\triangleq 1-(1+\mu)t,~~~z\triangleq\frac{R}{t_{\mu}^{\beta}}.

(1.20)

Assuming that solution
(\Omega,\Psi)
satisfies the self-similar structure as:

\displaystyle\Omega(t,R,\theta)=\frac{1}{t_{\mu}}F\left(z,\theta\right),~~~\Psi(t,R,\theta)=\frac{1}{t_{\mu}}\Phi\left(z,\theta\right),

(1.21)

then system (
1.2
) can be rewritten as the following self-similar form:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(1+\mu)F+(1+\mu)\beta D_{z}F+T=\mathcal{R}(\Phi)F\quad\text{(The main equation)},\\[4.30554pt]
L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)+L_{\theta}(\Phi)=F\quad\text{(The potential equation)},\\[4.30554pt]
F|_{\partial D}=0,~~\Phi|_{\partial D}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
(z,\theta)\in D
and
D\triangleq[0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
,

\displaystyle T

\displaystyle\triangleq U(\Phi)\partial_{\theta}F+V(\Phi)\alpha D_{z}F\quad\text{(The transport term)},

(1.25)

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\Phi)

\displaystyle\triangleq V(\Phi)-\tan\theta U(\Phi)\quad\text{(The stretching term)},

(1.26)

and the differential operators
L^{\alpha}_{z}
and
L_{\theta}
are defined by

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)=-\alpha^{2}D^{2}_{z}\Phi-5\alpha D_{z}\Phi,\quad L_{\theta}(\Phi)=-\partial_{\theta}V(\Phi)-6\Phi.

(1.27)

To be more specific,
L^{\alpha}_{z}
represents the radial operator, while
L_{\theta}
corresponds to the angular operator.
In this new frame of reference,
U(\Phi)
and
V(\Phi)
are reduced to

\displaystyle U(\Phi)=-3\Phi-\alpha D_{z}\Phi,~~V(\Phi)=\partial_{\theta}\Phi-(\tan\theta)\Phi.

(1.28)

One can see that the parameter
\beta_{0}
mentioned above is quantified by
\frac{\beta}{\alpha}
. Here and throughout this section, we process with a more intricate dissection of system (
1.3.2
) via the following two steps.

Step (i): The fundamental self-similar solution and its parameter stability.

To address this question, the first step in this work is to construct a family of solutions to the fundamental self-similar model parameterized by
\gamma
, and establish its parameter stability.
Specifically, we begin by analyzing the following fundamental self-similar model, which retains only the leading parts from system (
1.3.2
):

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\gamma D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~(z,\theta)\in D,\\[4.30554pt]
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}|_{\partial D}=0.\end{array}\right.

For the smooth (in
z
) solution
F^{\ast}_{1}
, Elgindi in
[
5
]
established the energy estimates of
F^{\ast}_{1}
in
\mathcal{W}^{l,\infty}
for any
l\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
Moreover, his result can be extended to any
\frac{1}{\gamma}\in\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
It is straightforward to verify that
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
fails to be smooth as
z
goes to
0
for
\frac{1}{\gamma}\notin\mathbb{N}^{+}
.
In this paper, we construct the Sobolev space
\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
(defined in Section
2
), and show that
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}\in\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
for any
\gamma>0
.
Moreover, we establish the parameter stability of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, a crucial step in determining a suitable
\gamma_{0}(\beta_{0})
for any given
\beta_{0}>0
.
A more detailed analysis is presented in Section
3
.

Step (ii): The energy estimate for the perturbation at critical regularity.

Based on the fundamental self-similar solution, we now decompose
F
as
F=F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+g
, then
g
satisfies the following self-similar system:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)=-T+R_{0}+R_{1}+R_{2},\\[4.30554pt]
L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)+L_{\theta}(\Phi)=F,\\[4.30554pt]
\mu=\frac{3}{4\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(R_{2}-T)(0),~~\gamma=\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu}\beta,\\[4.30554pt]
L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)(0)=0,~~g|_{\partial D}=0,~~\Phi|_{\partial D}=0,\end{array}\right.

where the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)
is defined by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)\triangleq g+\beta D_{z}g-\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})g-\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)F^{\ast}_{\gamma},

(1.35)

and the remaining terms are defined by

\displaystyle R_{0}\triangleq-\mu F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+(\gamma-(1+\mu)\beta)D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma},\quad R_{1}\triangleq-\mu g-\mu\beta D_{z}g,

(1.36)

\displaystyle R_{2}\triangleq\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)g-\frac{3}{2}(\sin\theta)^{2}\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}F+\mathcal{R}(\Phi-\Phi_{\text{main}})F.

(1.37)

Here,
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
contain the linear terms of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
g
, respectively, whereas
R_{2}
contains the nonlinear terms of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
g
.
Since
L^{-1}_{z,K}
is a non-local operator, it is not feasible to directly impose the condition
L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)(0)=0
, see
\eqref{1.4eq5}_{4}
(the definition of
L^{-1}_{z,K}
can be found in Section
2
below). Thus, we will ensure this boundary condition through selecting
\mu
, as in
\eqref{1.4eq5}_{3}
.
Another central step of this work is to establish the energy estimates at critical regularity, namely, in
\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
space.

In order to obtain the coercivity estimate for the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
, we select the suitable weights and an appropriate parameter
\eta
, then construct the Sobolev spaces
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
and
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\eta}
with
0<\beta\leq 1
, to establish coercivity estimate.
More precisely, we begin by decomposing the operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
as:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(f)+\tilde{P}(f),

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(f)\triangleq

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(f)-\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)F^{\ast}_{\beta}\quad\text{(the main operator)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(f)\triangleq

\displaystyle f+\beta D_{z}f-L_{z}^{-1}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})f\quad\text{( the core operator)},

\displaystyle\tilde{P}(f)\triangleq

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}-\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})f+\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)(F^{\ast}_{\beta}-F^{\ast}_{\gamma})\quad\text{(the perturbation term) }.

We then select an appropriate radial weight
w_{z}
, such that the core operator
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}
satisfies the following
L^{2}
-energy estimate:

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(g),g(w_{z})^{2}\rangle_{z}=\left(1-\frac{\beta}{2}\right)\|gw_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{z}}.

Based on the equality above, we choose a suitable angle weight
w^{K}_{\theta}
, such that the main operator
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}
satisfies

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(g),g(w^{K})^{2}\rangle=\|g\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}},

where
w^{K}\triangleq w_{z}w^{K}_{\theta}
and the definition of
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
is defined in Section
2
.
The construction of this new
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
space is one of the innovations in our paper.
Moreover, we apply the parameter stability of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
with respect to
\gamma
, to derive the parameter stability of the main operator.
This ensures that
\tilde{P}(g)
is indeed the perturbation term.
More details are shown in Section
3
and Section
5
.

By standard elliptic estimates, we can obtain that the
\mathcal{H}^{n}
-norm of
(\alpha^{2}D_{z}^{2}\Phi_{g},\partial_{\theta}^{2}\Phi_{g})
can be controlled by that of
g
, respectively.
Correspondingly, we also establish that the
\mathcal{H}^{n,\ast}
-norm of
(\alpha^{2}D_{z}^{2}\Phi_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}},\partial_{\theta}^{2}\Phi_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}})
can be bounded by that of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
.
Moreover, when establishing the elliptic estimates for
(\alpha^{2}D_{z}^{2}\Phi_{g},\partial_{\theta}^{2}\Phi_{g})
in
\mathcal{E}^{n}
, we need to introduce a new angle weight with the parameter
\lambda>1
. Thus, we derive the Hardy inequality with parameter
\xi>1
.
For more proof details, the reader can refer to Section
6
.

For the transport term
T
, when establishing the energy estimates in
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
, we define the quantity
(1-\eta)^{-1}\|D_{\theta}gw^{\lambda}\|_{L^{2}}
in
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\eta}
-norm to deal with its linear part.
By selecting
1-\eta
sufficiently small, we ensure that the
\mathcal{H}^{-1}
energy estimates remain controlled by the
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\eta}
-norm.
When developing energy estimate in
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\eta}
, we exploit the null structure present in
T
.
More precisely, we perform the decomposition of
T_{g}
as:

\displaystyle T_{g}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}-\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g,

where the notations
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}
and
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g
are defined by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}\triangleq\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}\left(\frac{U(\Phi)}{\sin(2\theta)}D_{\theta}(g\mathcal{W})+V(\Phi)\alpha D_{z}(g\mathcal{W})\right),\quad\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g\triangleq\left(\frac{U(\Phi)}{\sin(2\theta)}\frac{D_{\theta}\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}}+\alpha V(\Phi)\frac{D_{z}\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}}\right)g.

After some direct calculation, we get that

\displaystyle\big\langle T_{g\mathcal{W}},\big(g\mathcal{W}\big)z^{\frac{3}{\alpha}-1}\cos\theta\big\rangle=0.

(1.38)

By selecting a suitable
\mathcal{W}
, the main part of
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}
can be rewritten as:

\displaystyle\frac{D_{\theta}\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}}+\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2}=(1-2\xi)\cos(2\theta).

(1.39)

This indicates that the homogeneous part or even entire transport term
T
certainly is the perturbation of
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}
whenever
\xi
is approximately equal to
\frac{1}{2}
.
Accordingly, we refer to the structure (
1.38
) and the identity (
1.39
) with
\xi=\frac{1}{2}
as the null structure of transport term
T
.

This observation is key to establishing the energy estimates at critical regularity.
Thus, our choice of angle weights with the parameters
\eta
or
\lambda
allows us to treat additional principal terms as small perturbations relative to the linear part.
Consequently, in
\mathcal{H}^{2}_{\eta}
, we can obtain that

\displaystyle T_{g}=\text{Null~perturbation}+\text{Nonlinear~perturbation}.

Additionally, when handling
D_{z}D_{\theta}T_{g}
, there is a core difficulty to control the
L^{\infty}
-norm of
D_{z}\Big(\frac{U(\tilde{\Phi}_{g})}{\sin(2\theta)}\Big)
, we have to introduce the new norm of
D^{2}_{z}g
with an additional weight, which avoids increasing regularity. That is,

\displaystyle\bigg\|D_{z}\bigg(\frac{U(\tilde{\Phi}_{g})}{\sin(2\theta)}\bigg)\bigg\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim_{\beta}\alpha^{-1}\|g\|_{\mathcal{E}^{2}}.

Thus, we properly close the nonlinear energy estimates in
\mathcal{H}^{-1}\cap\mathcal{H}^{2}\cap\mathcal{E}^{2}
.
We point out that the discovery of the null structure and the introduction of the new space
\mathcal{E}^{2}
are key innovations in establishing the energy estimates at critical regularity in our paper.
Further discussion on the transport term
T
can be found in Section
7
.

We observe that
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
contain the linear terms, while
R_{2}
contains the nonlinear terms.
Through appropriate selection of small
\mu
and setting
\gamma=\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu}\beta
, we demonstrate that both
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
can indeed be regarded as small perturbations.
A complete proof is provided in Section
8
.

1.3.3 Conclusion

In conclusion, we demonstrate that the
L^{1}([0,t];L^{\infty}(D_{0}))
-norm of both
\omega
and
\frac{u_{r}}{r}
exhibits finite-time blow-up as
t
goes to a finite time
T^{\ast}
(see Theorem
1.1
). Furthermore, the time integrability of the solutions is rigorously examined. Specifically, it is shown that for any
p\in[1,\frac{2}{2-\beta})
, the velocity components
(u_{r},u_{3})\in L^{p}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}_{loc}(D_{0}))
. Moreover, adopting the parameter choice
\beta=1+\delta
with
0<\delta\ll 1
, Remark
1.2
shows the improved time integrability result
(u_{r},u_{3})\in L^{2}([0,T^{\ast}];L^{\infty}_{loc}(D_{0}))
. Collectively, these results provide a rigorous characterization of both the finite-time singularity formation and the integrability behavior of the self-similar solutions developed in this study.

Organization
.
In Section
2
, we set up some basic notations, which will be used extensively throughout this paper.
Section
3
presents our analysis of the fundamental model, establishing both parameter stability and energy estimates for the fundamental self-similar solutions.
Section
4
is devoted to asymptotic analysis of the self-similar system.
In Section
5
, we establish the coercivity estimate and the parameter stability of the main operator.
We derive the elliptic estimates of the solutions in Section
6
, while we treat the transport term in Section
7
.
Building upon these foundations, we show the blow-up phenomenon of the Euler equations in Section
8
. Specifically, we present complete proofs of Theorem
1.1
and Theorem
1.5
.

2
Notations

In this section, we present our notations that will be frequently used in the sequel.

(1)

We denote the usual Lebesgue
L^{p}
-norm of
u
by

\displaystyle\|u\|_{L^{p}(\Omega)}\triangleq\begin{cases}&\left(\int_{\Omega}|u|^{p}\ dx\right)^{\frac{1}{p}},\quad 1\leq p<\infty;\\
&\underset{x\in\Omega}{\operatorname{esssup}}|u(x)|,\quad p=\infty.\end{cases}

In addition, we use the
L^{p}_{\xi}
norm to denote the
L^{p}
norm with the independent variable
\xi=z~\text{or}~\theta
.

(2)

We use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
to denote the
L^{2}
inner product in
\Omega
,
\langle f,g\rangle\triangleq\int_{\Omega}f(x)g(x)dx
. Moreover, we use
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{\xi}
to denote the
L^{2}
inner product with the independent variable
\xi=z~\text{or}~\theta
.

(3)

We define the radial weights as:

w_{z}(z)\triangleq\frac{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\beta}})^{2}}{z^{\frac{2}{\beta}}},~~~~w^{\ast}_{z}(z)\triangleq\frac{1+z^{\frac{1}{\beta}}}{z^{\frac{1}{\beta}+\frac{1}{4}}},

and the angular weights as:

w^{K}_{\theta}(\theta)\triangleq(K(\theta))^{\frac{1}{2}(1-\frac{\alpha}{3\beta})},~~~~w^{\eta}_{\theta}(\theta)\triangleq(\sin(2\theta))^{-\frac{\eta}{2}},~~~~w^{\lambda}_{\theta}(\theta)\triangleq(\sin(2\theta))^{-\frac{\lambda}{2}}.

Then we take

w^{K}(z,\theta)\triangleq w^{K}_{\theta}\cdot w_{z},~~~~w^{\eta}(z,\theta)\triangleq w^{\eta}_{\theta}\cdot w_{z},~~~~w^{\lambda}(z,\theta)\triangleq w^{\lambda}_{\theta}\cdot w_{z},

and

w^{\ast,\eta}(z,\theta)\triangleq w^{\eta}_{\theta}\cdot w^{\ast}_{z},~~~~w^{\ast,\lambda}(z,\theta)\triangleq w^{\lambda}_{\theta}\cdot w^{\ast}_{z}.

(4)

We introduce the differential operators

D_{z}\triangleq z\partial_{z},~~~~D_{\theta}\triangleq\sin(2\theta)\partial_{\theta}.

Define the
\mathcal{H}^{-1}([0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
norm as:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}}

\displaystyle\triangleq\Big(1-\frac{\beta}{2}\Big)\|fw^{K}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\frac{1-\beta}{2c_{\ast,\beta}\beta}\|L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)w_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})^{\frac{1}{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{z}}

\displaystyle~~~+\frac{1}{2c_{\ast,\beta}\beta}\|L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)w_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}))^{\frac{1}{2}}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{z}}.

We also define the
\mathcal{H}^{k}([0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
norm as:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}\triangleq\sum_{i=0}^{k}\|D^{i}_{z}fw^{\eta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum_{0\leq i+j\leq k,j\geq 1}\|D^{i}_{z}D^{j}_{\theta}fw^{\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}},

and the
\mathcal{E}^{k}([0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
norm as:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{E}^{k}}\triangleq\alpha\left(\|fw^{\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\|D^{k}_{z}fw^{\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\right).

Moreover, we take the
\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}([0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
norm as:

\displaystyle\|f\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}}\triangleq\sum_{i=0}^{k}\|D^{i}_{z}fw^{\ast,\eta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}+\sum_{0\leq i+j\leq k,j\geq 1}\|D^{i}_{z}D^{j}_{\theta}fw^{\ast,\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}.

(5)

Define the integral operators

L^{-1}_{z}(f)(z)\triangleq\int_{z}^{\infty}\frac{f(\rho)}{\rho}d\rho,

and

L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)(z)\triangleq\int_{z}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}f(\rho,\theta)\frac{K(\theta)}{\rho}d\theta d\rho.

Note that
D_{z}(L^{-1}_{z}(f))=-f(z)
and
D_{z}(L^{-1}_{z,K}(f))=-\langle f,K\rangle_{\theta}
.

(6)

We use the notation
a\lesssim b
(resp.
a\gtrsim b
) to indicate that there exists a uniform constant
C
, which may be different in each occurrence, such that
a\leq Cb
(resp.
a\geq Cb
). Moreover, the symbol
a\approx b
represents
a\lesssim b\lesssim a
.
We also use the notation
a\leq C_{d}b
to emphasize that the constant
C_{d}
depends on some parameter
d
.
Sometimes we will write
a\lesssim_{d}b
if we want to emphasize that the implicit constant depends on some parameter
d
.

3
The fundamental model

Before investigating the blow-up phenomenon of the Euler equations (
1.1
), we first consider the fundamental model in this section.
More precisely, we will study the blow-up phenomenon of the fundamental model and the parameter stability of the fundamental self-similar model.
The stability of the parameters plays the crucial role in fixing the scaling index of the solutions of the Euler equations (
1.1
).

3.1.  Blow-up phenomenon of the fundamental model

Firstly, we study the following fundamental model as:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\partial_{t}\Omega^{\ast}=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{R,K}(\Omega^{\ast})\Omega^{\ast},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~(R,\theta)\in D,\\[4.30554pt]
\Omega^{\ast}|_{\partial D}=0.\end{array}\right.

where
D\triangleq[0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and
L^{-1}_{R,K}(\Omega^{\ast})(R)\triangleq\int_{R}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}\Omega^{\ast}(\rho,\theta)\frac{K(\theta)}{\rho}d\theta d\rho
.

Proposition 3.1
.

Assume that
\Gamma(\theta)
satisfies
\Gamma(0)=\Gamma(\frac{\pi}{2})=0
,
K(\theta)\Gamma(\theta)\in L^{1}([0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
and

\displaystyle c_{\ast}\triangleq\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}K(\theta)\Gamma(\theta)d\theta\neq 0.

Let
\alpha>0
,
\gamma>0
, then the fundamental model (
3.1
) admits a family of self-similar solutions of the form

\displaystyle\Omega^{\ast}(R,\theta,t)=\frac{2\alpha}{3(1-t)}\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{c_{\ast}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}\left(\frac{R}{(1-t)^{\gamma}}\right),

(3.3)

where
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)\triangleq\frac{2z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{\gamma(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{2}}
.

Proof.

Taking
\overline{\Omega}^{\ast}\triangleq\frac{3}{2\alpha}\Omega^{\ast}
, we deduce from (
3.1
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{t}\overline{\Omega}^{\ast}=L^{-1}_{R,K}(\overline{\Omega}^{\ast})\overline{\Omega}^{\ast}.

(3.4)

By virtue of the separated variable method, we consider
\overline{\Omega}^{\ast}
as the following form:

\displaystyle\overline{\Omega}^{\ast}(t,R,\theta)=\frac{1}{1-t}\frac{\Gamma(\theta)}{c_{\ast}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z),

(3.5)

where
z\triangleq\frac{R}{(1-t)^{\gamma}}
.
For any
\Gamma(\theta)
, which satisfies
\Gamma(0)=\Gamma(\frac{\pi}{2})=0
and
K(\theta)\Gamma(\theta)\in L^{1}([0,\frac{\pi}{2}])
, we derive from (
3.4
) that
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\gamma D_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}=L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma},

(3.6)

where
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)
is defined by

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)\triangleq\int_{z}^{\infty}\frac{\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(\rho)}{\rho}d\rho.

(3.7)

We will proceed to solve the ordinary differential equation (
3.6
) with (
3.7
).

By virtue of (
3.7
), it is easy to check that

\displaystyle D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)=-\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z).

(3.8)

The combination of (
3.6
) and (
3.8
) leads us to get that

\displaystyle D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-\gamma D_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}=-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}.

(3.9)

Without loss of generality, we consider
\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow+\infty}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)=0
.
It then follows from (
3.9
) that

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-\gamma\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=\int_{z}^{\infty}L^{-1}_{\rho}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\frac{\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}}{\rho}d\rho=\int_{z}^{\infty}L^{-1}_{\rho}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(-\partial_{\rho}L^{-1}_{\rho}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}))d\rho=\frac{1}{2}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}))^{2}.

(3.10)

Again thanks to (
3.8
), one can deduce from (
3.10
) that

\displaystyle\gamma z\partial_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\left(\frac{1}{2}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1\right)L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}).

(3.11)

One can see that (
3.11
) is an ordinary differential equation of
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})
.
It is easy to check that
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\equiv 0
and
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\equiv 2
are two trivial solutions of (
3.11
).
Then
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)=-D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\equiv 0
for all
z\in(0,+\infty)
.
We find that
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\equiv 2
and
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)\equiv 0
do not satisfy (
3.7
).
Thus, only
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)\equiv 0
is the trivial solution for the ordinary equation (
3.6
) with (
3.7
).
But we do not consider the trivial solution here.

Now, we aim to find the non-trivial solution for the ordinary equation (
3.6
) with (
3.7
).
After some direct calculations, (
3.11
) can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle\left(\frac{1}{L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-2}-\frac{1}{L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})}\right)\partial_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\frac{1}{\gamma z}.

(3.12)

Next, we will discuss and solve the ordinary differential equation (
3.12
) based on the range of
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})
.

Case 1: For
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)<0
, it follows from (
3.12
) that

\displaystyle\partial_{z}\ln\left(\frac{2-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})}{-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})}\right)=\partial_{z}\ln z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}},

which implies that

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)=\frac{2}{1-cz^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\forall~z\in(0,+\infty),

with some constant
c>0
.
For
z\in(0,c^{-\gamma})
,
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)>0
.
This contradicts
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})<0
.

Case 2: For
0<L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)<2
, applying the similar method, we obtain that the solution of (
3.12
) is

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)=\frac{2}{1+cz^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\forall~z\in(0,+\infty),

(3.13)

where
c>0
and

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)=-D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\frac{2cz^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{\gamma(1+cz^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{2}},\quad\forall~z\in(0,+\infty).

(3.14)

Case 3: For
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)>2
, we can also arrive at the following conclusion through a similar derivation as:

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\frac{2}{1-cz^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\forall~z\in(0,+\infty),

with some constant
c>0
.
For
z\in(c^{-\gamma},+\infty)
,
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)<0
. This contradicts
L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)>2
.

Without loss of generality, we take
c=1
in (
3.13
) and (
3.14
), and obtain that for any
z\in(0,+\infty)
,

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)=\frac{2}{1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}},\quad\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)=-D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})=\frac{2z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{\gamma(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{2}}.

(3.15)

Substituting (
3.15
) into (
3.5
), we can obtain (
3.3
) directly.
We thus complete the proof of this proposition.
∎

3.2.  Parameter stability and energy estimate of the fundamental self-similar solutions

In this subsection, we consider the fundamental self-similar model as follows:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\gamma D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma},\quad t\in\mathbb{R}^{+},~~(z,\theta)\in D,\\[4.30554pt]
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}|_{\partial D}=0,\end{array}\right.

where
D\triangleq[0,\infty)\times[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]
and
L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})(z)\triangleq\int_{z}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}(\rho,\theta)\frac{K(\theta)}{\rho}d\theta d\rho
.

Firstly, we study the parameter stability of the fundamental self-similar solutions for equation (
3.2
).

Proposition 3.2
.

Let
0<\alpha\leq\gamma
, then the fundamental self-similar model (
3.2
) admits the solutions of the form

\displaystyle F^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z,\theta)=\frac{2\alpha}{3}\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z),

(3.18)

where
c_{\ast,\gamma}
,
\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)
and
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)
are defined by

\displaystyle c_{\ast,\gamma}\triangleq\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}K(\theta)\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)d\theta,\quad\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}(\theta)\triangleq(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}},\quad\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(z)\triangleq\frac{2z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{\gamma(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{2}}.

(3.19)

Moreover, for

\gamma_{1},~\gamma_{2}\in(0,2)
and
\delta_{0}\in(0,1)
, if
|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}|\leq\min\{\frac{\ln(1-\delta_{0})}{2\ln(\delta_{0})},\delta_{0}\}
, then there holds

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2\alpha}\|F^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}-F^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}\leq C\frac{\delta_{0}}{\gamma_{1}}.

(3.20)

Proof.

By virtue of Proposition
3.1
, we conclude that (
3.18
) holds immediately.
Next, we intend to prove (
3.20
).
If
|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}|\leq\frac{\ln(1-\delta_{0})}{2\ln(\delta_{0})}
, for any
z\in[0,1]
, we claim that

\displaystyle|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq\delta_{0}.

(3.21)

Without loss of generality,
we assume that
\gamma_{1}\leq\gamma_{2}
, and the proof of the other case with
\gamma_{1}>\gamma_{2}
is similar.
For any
z\in[0,1]
, we have

\displaystyle|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}(1-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}).

If
z\leq\delta^{2}_{0}
, then we get

\displaystyle|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}\leq\delta_{0}.

(3.22)

Note that
1-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}
is a monotonically decreasing function. If
z\geq\delta^{2}_{0}
, then we obtain that

\displaystyle|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq 1-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}\leq 1-(\delta_{0})^{2(\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}})}\leq 1-(\delta_{0})^{\frac{\ln(1-\delta_{0})}{\ln(\delta_{0})}}=\delta_{0}.

(3.23)

The combination of (
3.22
) and (
3.23
) shows that claim (
3.21
) is valid.

With the help of (
3.15
) and (
3.21
), one deduce that for any
z\in[0,1]
,

\displaystyle|L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})|=\frac{2}{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}})(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}})}|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

For another range that
z\geq 1
, we get that

\displaystyle|L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})|\leq\frac{2}{z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}}|z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-z^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq 2|(z^{-1})^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}}-(z^{-1})^{\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}}|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

Then we conclude that for any
z\geq 0
, there holds

\displaystyle|L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})|\leq 2\delta_{0}.

(3.24)

According to (
3.10
), (
3.15
) and (
3.24
), we find that for any
|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}|\leq\delta_{0}
,

\displaystyle|\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}-\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}}|\lesssim

\displaystyle\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})\right|+\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\left(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})\right)^{2}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\left(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})\right)^{2}\right|

(3.25)

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\left|L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})-L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})\right|+\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\right||L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})|

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\left|\left(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}})]^{2}-[L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})\right)^{2}\right|+\left|\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}-\frac{1}{\gamma_{2}}\right|\left|L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}})\right|^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\gamma_{1}}\delta_{0},

owing to
0<\gamma_{1},\gamma_{2}<2
.
Moreover, we infer from (
3.21
) and
(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3}}\in[0,1]
that

\displaystyle|c_{\ast,\gamma_{1}}-c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}|\lesssim\sup_{\theta\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]}|\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{1}}-\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{2}}|\lesssim\sup_{\theta\in[0,\frac{\pi}{2}]}|(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma_{1}}}-(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma_{2}}}|\lesssim\delta_{0}.

(3.26)

In view of (
3.25
) and (
3.26
), we conclude that

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2\alpha}\|F^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}-F^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}}\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle=\left\|\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{1}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{1}}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}(z)-\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{2}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}}(z)\right\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left\|\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{1}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{1}}}-\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{2}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}(z)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{2}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}(z)-\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{2}}(z)\|_{L^{\infty}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\left\|\frac{(c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}-c_{\ast,\gamma_{1}})\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{1}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{1}}c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}+\left\|\frac{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{1}}(\theta)-\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma_{2}}(\theta)}{c_{\ast,\gamma_{2}}}\right\|_{L^{\infty}}\right)\|\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma_{1}}(z)\|_{L^{\infty}}+\frac{\delta_{0}}{\gamma_{1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\delta_{0}}{\gamma_{1}}.

Therefore, we finish the proof of this proposition.
∎

In the last of this subsection, we intend to study the
\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}
norm of the fundamental self-similar solutions
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and give the following lemma.

Lemma 3.3
.

Let
\beta\in(0,1]
and
\gamma=\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu}\beta
.
There exist constants
\alpha>0
sufficiently small and
\eta(\beta)
, such that if
\alpha\ll 1-\eta\ll\beta
and
|\mu|\leq\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}
, then there holds

\displaystyle\|F^{\ast}_{\gamma}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k,\ast}}\leq C_{\beta}\alpha(1-\eta)^{-\frac{1}{2}},

where integer
k
satisfies
0\leq k\leq 4
.

Proof.

Firstly, by some direct calculations, we find that for any
0<a<1
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}(\sin(2\theta))^{-a}d\theta\approx(1-a)^{-1},

(3.27)

which will be frequently used.
We can deduce that

\displaystyle|\gamma-\beta|=\frac{2|\mu|}{1-\mu}\beta\lesssim\alpha^{\frac{1}{2}}\ll 1.

(3.28)

Recalling (
1.18
), (
3.18
) and (
3.19
), then applying (
3.27
), (
3.28
) and
K(\theta)\leq\sin(2\theta)
, one gets that

\displaystyle\|F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\eta}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\right)^{2}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}(\sin(2\theta))^{\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}-\eta}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}}{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{4}}\frac{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\beta}})^{2}}{z^{\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}}}dz

(3.29)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{2}(1-\eta)^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z^{\frac{3}{4}}(1+z)}dz\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{2}(1-\eta)^{-1}.

We note that, since
\eta\in(0,1)
, we use
\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}>0
, then apply (
3.27
) to directly obtain the second inequality in (
3.29
).
By virtue of (
3.8
), (
3.9
) and (
3.15
), we see that

\displaystyle|D_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{\gamma}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma},

(3.30)

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)^{2}-\frac{1}{\gamma}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})^{2}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma^{2}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}.

(3.31)

Thanks to (
3.30
) and (
3.31
), it then follows from a similar way as (
3.29
) that

\displaystyle\|D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\gamma^{4}}(1-\eta)^{-1}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{4}}(1-\eta)^{-1},~~~\|D^{2}_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{6}}(1-\eta)^{-1}.

Remembering the definition of
K(\theta)
in (
1.18
), we infer that

\displaystyle|D_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}-1}D_{\theta}K(\theta)\right|=\left|\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})\right|\lesssim\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma},

(3.32)

\displaystyle|D^{2}_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}\left(\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{2}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})^{2}-\frac{2\alpha}{\gamma}\sin^{2}(2\theta)\right)\right|\lesssim\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}\left(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}+\sin(2\theta)\right).

(3.33)

We recall the definition of
\lambda
in (
1.12
), then use the fact that
K(\theta)\leq\sin(2\theta)
and (
3.32
), to arrive at

\displaystyle\|D_{\theta}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\right)^{4}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}(\sin(2\theta))^{\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}-\lambda}d\theta\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{z^{\frac{2}{\gamma}}}{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}})^{4}}\frac{(1+z^{\frac{1}{\beta}})^{2}}{z^{\frac{2}{\beta}+\frac{1}{2}}}dz

(3.34)

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{3}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{1}{z^{\frac{3}{4}}(1+z)}dz\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{2}.

We point out that since
\lambda>1
, we can not drop out
\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}
directly. But we have

\displaystyle\lambda-\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}=1-\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\frac{17-23\mu}{30(1+\mu)}\in(0,1).

(3.35)

Thus, we can apply (
3.27
) to obtain the second inequality in (
3.34
).

The same manner yields immediately

\displaystyle\|D^{2}_{\theta}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{2}.

According to separate variables of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, the estimates (
3.30
) and (
3.32
), we can deduce from a similar way as (
3.29
) and (
3.34
) that

\displaystyle\|D_{z}D_{\theta}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\lambda}\|^{2}_{L^{2}}\lesssim\left(\frac{\alpha}{\beta}\right)^{2}.

After some straightforward calculations, one can get that

\displaystyle|D^{3}_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{\gamma^{3}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)^{3}-\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})^{2}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma^{3}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma},

(3.36)

and

\displaystyle|D^{3}_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}|

\displaystyle\leq[K(\theta)]^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}\left|\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{3}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})^{3}-9\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{2}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})\sin^{2}(2\theta)\right.

(3.37)

\displaystyle~~~\left.+\frac{8\alpha}{\gamma}\cos(2\theta)\sin^{2}(2\theta)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}\sin(2\theta).

Owing to the separate variables of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, we infer from the estimates (
3.30
)-(
3.33
), (
3.36
) and (
3.37
) that

\displaystyle\|D^{3}_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}w^{\ast,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{i+j=3,j\geq 1}\|D^{i}_{z}D^{j}_{\theta}fw^{\ast,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{8}}(1-\eta)^{-1}.

Finally, it follows from some direct computations that

\displaystyle|D^{4}_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}|

\displaystyle=\left|\frac{1}{\gamma^{4}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)^{4}-\frac{11}{\gamma^{3}}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})^{2}(L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})-1)^{2}+\frac{4}{\gamma^{2}}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})^{3}\right|\lesssim\frac{1}{\gamma^{4}}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma},

(3.38)

and

\displaystyle|D^{4}_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}|

\displaystyle\leq(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}\left|\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{4}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})^{4}-18\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{3}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})^{2}\sin^{2}(2\theta)\right.

(3.39)

\displaystyle~~~+27\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{2}\sin^{4}(2\theta)-24\left(\frac{2\alpha}{3\gamma}\right)^{2}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})\sin^{2}(2\theta)\cos(2\theta)

\displaystyle~~~\left.+\frac{32\alpha}{\gamma}\sin^{2}(2\theta)\cos^{2}(2\theta)-\frac{16\alpha}{\gamma}\sin^{4}(2\theta)\right|

\displaystyle\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\gamma^{2}}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}+\frac{\alpha}{\gamma}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}\sin(2\theta).

Due to the separate variables of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, we deduce from the estimates (
3.30
)-(
3.33
) and (
3.36
)-(
3.39
) that

\displaystyle\|D^{4}_{z}fw^{\ast,\eta}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}+\sum_{i+j=4,j\geq 1}\|D^{i}_{z}D^{j}_{\theta}fw^{\ast,\lambda}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\lesssim\frac{\alpha^{2}}{\beta^{10}}(1-\eta)^{-1}.

The proof of this lemma is thus completed.
∎

4
Self-similar variables and asymptotic analysis

In this section, we aim to investigate the backward self-similar solution of system (
1.2
). For any given
\beta\in(0,1]
and
\mu\in\mathbb{R}
that would be determined later, we consider the following system:

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}(1+\mu)F+(1+\mu)\beta D_{z}F+T=\mathcal{R}(\Phi)F,\quad(z,\theta)\in D,\\[4.30554pt]
L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)+L_{\theta}(\Phi)=F,\\[4.30554pt]
F|_{\partial D}=0,~~\Phi|_{\partial D}=0,\end{array}\right.

where the transport term
T
and the stretching term
\mathcal{R}(\Phi)
are defined by (
1.25
) and (
1.26
), respectively.
The differential operators
L^{\alpha}_{z}
and
L_{\theta}
are defined by (
1.27
).
Here and throughout this section, we perform a refined analysis of system (
4
).

4.1.  Decomposition of potential equation

In this subsection, we focus on the potential equation as:

\displaystyle L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)+L_{\theta}(\Phi)=F,

and decompose
F
based on the structure and properties of
L^{\alpha}_{z}
and
L_{\theta}
.
It is worth highlighting that the operators
L^{\alpha}_{z}
and
L_{\theta}
are to some extent independent.
Therefore, it is natural to investigate the properties of these two operators separately.

(1) Analysis of
L^{\alpha}_{z}

We point out that obtaining estimates of
\alpha D_{z}\Phi
from the elliptic operator
L^{\alpha}_{z}(\Phi)
is of critical importance.
Let
f\in C[0,\infty)
, we want to find
G(f)\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
, which vanishes as
z
goes to infinity, such that

L^{\alpha}_{z}(G(f))=f.

Indeed, by some direct computations,
L^{\alpha}_{z}(G(f))
can be rewritten as:

L^{\alpha}_{z}(G(f))=-\alpha^{2}D_{z}\Big(z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}D_{z}\big(z^{\frac{5}{\alpha}}G(f)\big)\Big).

It is easy to compute that

\displaystyle f=-D_{z}L^{-1}_{z}(f).

(4.4)

Hence, we just need to solve

\displaystyle\alpha^{2}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}D_{z}(z^{\frac{5}{\alpha}}G(f))=L^{-1}_{z}(f),

(4.5)

where
L^{-1}_{z}(f)\in C^{1}(0,\infty)
vanishes as
z\rightarrow\infty
.
Return to (
4.5
), we assume that
L^{-1}_{\rho}(f)\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}}=o(1)
as
\rho\rightarrow 0
, then employ (
4.4
) and integrate by parts, to get that

\displaystyle G(f)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\alpha^{2}}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{z}\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}-1}L^{-1}_{\rho}(f)d\rho

(4.6)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{5\alpha}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}\left(L^{-1}_{\rho}(f)\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}}\right)\bigg|_{\rho=0}^{\rho=z}+\frac{1}{5\alpha}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{z}\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}-1}fd\rho

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{5\alpha}L^{-1}_{z}(f)+\frac{1}{5\alpha}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{z}\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}-1}fd\rho,

\displaystyle\triangleq G^{*}(f)+\widetilde{G}(f).

Taking
f(0)=0
, we infer that

\lim_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}\widetilde{G}(f)=\frac{1}{25}\lim_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}f(z)=0,\quad\lim_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}G^{*}(f)=\frac{1}{5\alpha}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{f(\rho)}{\rho}d\rho.

We note that the following limit, in general, does not vanish.
But we can get that

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}\left|\frac{\widetilde{G}(f)}{G^{*}(f)}\right|\lesssim_{f}\alpha.

(4.7)

Moreover, one can verify that

\displaystyle\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{G}(f)}{G^{*}(f)}=\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\widetilde{G}(f)}{f}\lim_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{f}{G^{*}(f)}=-\frac{\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{D_{z}f}{f}}{\frac{5}{\alpha}+\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{D_{z}f}{f}}.

(4.8)

The estimates (
4.7
) and (
4.8
) imply the crucial inequality below

\max\limits_{z\in[0,\infty)}\left|\frac{\widetilde{G}(f)}{G^{*}(f)}\right|\lesssim_{f}\alpha.

In conclusion, we decompose
G(f)
into
G^{*}(f)
and
\widetilde{G}(f)
.
Here,
G^{*}(f)
constitutes the main component, while
G(f)
can be regarded as a perturbation of
G^{*}(f)
.

(2) Analysis of
L_{\theta}

It is essential to derive estimates for
\partial_{\theta}\Phi
from the elliptic operator
L_{\theta}(\Phi)
.
Denote
L^{*}_{\theta}
as the conjugate operator of
L_{\theta}
. Then we obtain that

\langle L_{\theta}(h),f\rangle_{\theta}=\langle h,L^{*}_{\theta}(f)\rangle_{\theta},~~~\forall~f,~h\in C^{2}\left(0,\frac{\pi}{2}\right).

In fact, the operator
L^{*}_{\theta}
can be specifically written as

\displaystyle L^{*}_{\theta}=-\partial_{\theta}^{2}-(\tan\theta)\partial_{\theta}-6.

And it is easy to verify that

\displaystyle L_{\theta}(\sin(2\theta))=L^{*}_{\theta}(K(\theta))=0.

(4.9)

For any given
f\in C[0,\infty)
, we consider the following model:

L_{\theta}(h)=f,

where
h\in C^{2}(0,\infty)
.
We can derive that the following necessary condition holds:

\langle f,K\rangle_{\theta}=\langle L_{\theta}(h),K\rangle_{\theta}=\langle h,L^{*}_{\theta}(K)\rangle_{\theta}=0,

which indicates that
f
should be orthogonal to
K(\theta)
. However, it is unrealistic to require
\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}=0
.
In order to solve the difficulty above, we regard
L^{\alpha}_{z}+L_{\theta}
as a perturbation of
L^{\alpha}_{z}
, which would inherit the pleasing estimates from
L_{\theta}
.

(3) Analysis of
L^{\alpha}_{z}+L_{\theta}

We aim to find
\tilde{F}
, such that
\langle\tilde{F},K\rangle_{\theta}=0
.
Firstly, we compute that

\displaystyle\langle\sin(2\theta),K\rangle_{\theta}=\frac{4}{15}.

So, we consider the decomposition of
F
as follows:

F=\bar{F}+\tilde{F},~~~\bar{F}\triangleq\frac{15}{4}\sin(2\theta)\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta},~~~\tilde{F}\triangleq F-\frac{15}{4}\sin(2\theta)\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}.

Hence,
\bar{F}
and
\tilde{F}
satisfy

\langle\bar{F},K\rangle_{\theta}=\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta},~~~\langle\tilde{F},K\rangle_{\theta}=0.

Correspondingly, recalling (
4.9
), we can split
\Phi
as follows:

\Phi=G(z)\sin(2\theta)+\widetilde{\Phi},

where
G
and
\widetilde{\Phi}
satisfy

L^{\alpha}_{z}(G)=\frac{15}{4}\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta},~~~L^{\alpha}_{z}(\widetilde{\Phi})+L_{\theta}(\widetilde{\Phi})=\widetilde{F},

In the above decomposition, we observe that
\widetilde{\Phi}
deserves better estimates since
\langle\tilde{F},K\rangle_{\theta}=0
.
According to (
4.6
), by taking
f=\frac{15}{4}\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}
, we can further decompose
\Phi
into the following three parts:

\displaystyle\Phi=\Phi_{\text{main}}+\widetilde{G}\sin(2\theta)+\widetilde{\Phi},

(4.10)

where
\Phi_{\text{main}}
and
\widetilde{G}
are defined by

\Phi_{\text{main}}\triangleq G^{\ast}\sin(2\theta),~~~G^{\ast}\triangleq\frac{3}{4\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F),~~~\widetilde{G}\triangleq\frac{3}{4\alpha}z^{-\frac{5}{\alpha}}\int_{0}^{z}\rho^{\frac{5}{\alpha}-1}\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}d\rho.

We note that
\widetilde{G}
and
\widetilde{\Phi}
defined above both deserve better estimates than
\Phi_{\text{main}}
.
Thus, in the remaining analysis of this section, we will treat
\Phi
as a perturbation of
\Phi_{\text{main}}
, which is a crucial step in our approach.

4.2.  Asymptotic analysis of stretching term

The prevailing belief is that solutions to the 3D Euler equations blow up in finite time, as few global existence results are known.
And the stretching term
\mathcal{R}(\Phi)F
in system (
4
) definitely causes blow-up.
Our goal in this subsection is to derive the profile that generates the blow-up from the stretching term.
As shown in (
4.10
), we treat
\Phi
as the perturbation of
\Phi_{\text{main}}
and compute that

\displaystyle\mathcal{R}(\Phi)=\mathcal{R}(\Phi_{\text{main}})+\mathcal{R}(\Phi-\Phi_{\text{main}})=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F)-\frac{3}{2}\sin^{2}\theta\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}+\mathcal{R}(\Phi-\Phi_{\text{main}}).

Hence,
\eqref{4eq3}_{1}
can be written as follows:

\displaystyle(1+\mu)F+(1+\mu)\beta D_{z}F+T=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F)F-\frac{3}{2}\sin^{2}\theta\langle F,K\rangle_{\theta}F+\mathcal{R}(\Phi-\Phi_{\text{main}})F.

(4.11)

A natural idea is to regard (
4.11
) as a perturbation of the following fundamental self-similar model:

\displaystyle F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\gamma D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma},~~~\forall~\gamma\in(0,\infty),

(4.12)

which has been extensively studied in Section
3
. According to Proposition
3.2
, the fundamental self-similar model (
4.12
) admits a solution
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
.
Then, we rewrite
F
as a perturbation of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
, that is

\displaystyle F=F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+g,

(4.13)

where
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
is regarded as the main part of
F
and
g
is regarded as the perturbation of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
.
This, along with (
4.11
) and (
4.12
), leads us to derive that
g
satisfies the following equation:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)=-T+R_{0}+R_{1}+R_{2},

(4.14)

where the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)
is defined by (
1.35
),
and the remaining terms are defined by (
1.36
)-(
1.37
).
Here,
R_{0}
and
R_{1}
contain the linear terms of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
g
, respectively, while
R_{2}
contains the nonlinear terms of
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
and
g
.
We point out that we will use different way to deal with these three terms.

According to the theory of stability analysis, we know that linear terms determine the nature of the partial differential equations.
Therefore, our core task is to derive the coercivity estimate from the linear operator.
For simplicity, we define the core part of the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(g)

\displaystyle\triangleq g+\beta D_{z}g-\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\beta})g\quad\text{( the core operator)},

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(g)

\displaystyle\triangleq\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(g)-\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)F^{\ast}_{\beta}\quad\text{(the main operator)}.

Actually, the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)
can be rewritten in the following perturbation form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)=\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(g)+\text{Linear perturbation}.

(4.15)

(1) Analysis of the core operator
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}

By choosing the suitable weight, we can obtain the following coercivity estimate:

\langle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(g),g(w_{z})^{2}\rangle_{z}=\left(1-\frac{\beta}{2}\right)\|gw_{z}\|^{2}_{L^{2}_{z}}.

Reader may refer to Proposition
5.1
for more details.
In other words, it is necessary to establish the linear/nonlinear stability of
g
in
L^{2}_{z}
with weight
w_{z}
satisfying

w_{z}=O(z^{-\frac{2}{\beta}}),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow 0^{+};~~~w_{z}=O(1),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow\infty.

This observation motivates us to construct
g
with the following radial contour:

\displaystyle g=o(z^{\frac{2}{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow 0^{+};~~~g=o(z^{-\frac{1}{2}}),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow\infty.

(4.16)

If
g
satisfies (
4.16
), then for convenience, we denote that

\displaystyle g=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

(4.17)

We note that if (
4.17
) holds, then for
0<\beta\leq 1
,
g
satisfies
\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow 0^{+}}\frac{g(z)}{\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}(z)}=0.

But we can not directly deduce from (
4.17
) that for
0<\beta\leq 1
,

\lim\limits_{z\rightarrow\infty}\frac{g(z)}{\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}(z)}=0.

Thus, we refer to the notation
g=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})
as indicating that
g
is a stable disturbance of
\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}
at the origin.

(2) Analysis of the main operator
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}

We observe that
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(g)
contains an additional term
\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)F^{\ast}_{\beta}
compared to
\mathcal{L}^{\beta}(g)
.
Thus, in what follows, we only need to estimate
\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)F^{\ast}_{\beta}
.
As a component of linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
, we still need to ensure

\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)F^{\ast}_{\beta}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}),

which requires that

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)=o(z^{\frac{1}{\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow 0^{+}.

(4.18)

Therefore,
g
needs to meet the additional structural condition:

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)(0)=\int_{0}^{\infty}\int_{0}^{\frac{\pi}{2}}g(\rho,\theta)\frac{K(\theta)}{\rho}d\theta d\rho=0.

(4.19)

However, since the operator
L^{-1}_{z,K}
is non-local, the structural condition (
4.19
) cannot be derived from the equation (
4.14
) naturally and directly.
Therefore, we introduce the parameter
\mu
to ensure that (
4.19
) is satisfied, which will be detailed in Subsection
4.4
.
With (
4.19
) in hand, a new weight
w^{K}
is introduced and the coercivity estimate of low frequency is obtained in Proposition
5.2
. Specifically,

\langle\mathcal{L}^{\beta}_{\Gamma}(g),g(w^{K})^{2}\rangle=\|g\|^{2}_{\mathcal{H}^{-1}},

which implies the better property for the low frequency of
L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)
:

\displaystyle L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)=o(z^{\frac{3}{2\beta}-\frac{1}{2}}),~~~\text{as}~z\rightarrow 0^{+}.

(4.20)

We emphasize that since there exists the difference between
L^{-1}_{z,K}(g)
and other components in
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
, the coercivity estimate of
g
at low frequencies plays a crucial role in closing the estimate for linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}
in
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-norm.

(3) Analysis of
R_{0}

According to theory of nonlinear stability analysis, it is essential to show that all terms on the right-hand side of (
4.14
) are of the order
O(g)
. Given the assumptions regarding the perturbation terms, it is readily apparent that
R_{1}
and
R_{2}
both meet this requirement, except for
R_{0}
.
Consequently, a more detailed analysis of
R_{0}
is warranted.
Indeed, we deduce from (
4.12
) that

\displaystyle\frac{R_{0}}{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=-\mu+(\gamma-(1+\mu)\beta)\frac{D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}=(1+\mu)\left(-1+\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\right)+\left(1-(1+\mu)\frac{\beta}{\gamma}\right)\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma}).

(4.21)

According to Proposition
3.2
and (
3.15
), we have

\displaystyle\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})(0)=L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})(0)=2.

(4.22)

Collecting (
4.21
) and (
4.22
) together, we find that

\displaystyle\frac{R_{0}}{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\bigg|_{z=0}=1-\mu-(1+\mu)\frac{\beta}{\gamma}.

We choose

\displaystyle\gamma=\frac{1+\mu}{1-\mu}\beta,

(4.23)

which implies that

\frac{R_{0}}{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\Big|_{z=0}=0.

With (
4.23
) at hand, if we choose
|\mu|\ll\beta\leq 1
, then
R_{0}
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle R_{0}=\mu\big(-2+\frac{3}{2\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})\big)F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=-\frac{2\mu z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

(4.24)

(4) Conclusion

In view of (
4.17
), (
4.20
) and (
4.24
), we finally conclude that

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}),~~~R_{0}+R_{1}+R_{2}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

(4.25)

4.3.  The null structure of transport term

Our goal in this subsection is to guarantee the ingredients appearing in the transport term
T
are of
\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})
.
The divergence free condition is well-known as a fundamental structure for the incompressible Euler equations (
1
)-(
1.6
) and plays a crucial role in its regularity-related issues.
Therefore, our next goal is to explore the relevant null structure under new variables and construct compatible Banach spaces to establish the global existence of the self-similar system (
4
) with critical regularity.
Recalling the decomposition (
4.13
), then transport term
T
can be rewritten as follows:

\displaystyle T=T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}+T_{g},

(4.26)

where for any given
f
, which denotes either
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
or
g
, the notation
T_{f}
is defined by

T_{f}\triangleq\frac{U(\Phi)}{\sin(2\theta)}D_{\theta}f+V(\Phi)\alpha D_{z}f.

(1) Analysis of
T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}

Based on the analysis of the linear operator
\mathcal{L}_{\Gamma}(g)
in (
4.25
), we need to ensure that
T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta})
.
For any given
f
, which denotes either
F^{\ast}_{\gamma}
or
g
, we denote that
G^{\ast}_{f}\triangleq\frac{3}{4\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(f)
.
According to (
4.10
) and (
4.13
), we can treat
\Phi
as the perturbation of
G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta)
.
By some direct calculations, one obtains that

\displaystyle U(\Phi)

\displaystyle=U(G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))+U(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))

(4.27)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{4\alpha}\sin(2\theta)L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})+\frac{\alpha}{2}\sin(2\theta)\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}+U(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta)),

and

\displaystyle V(\Phi)

\displaystyle=V(G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))+V(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))

(4.28)

\displaystyle=\frac{3}{2\alpha}(\cos(2\theta)-\sin^{2}\theta)L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})+V(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta)).

Then, we can decompose
T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
as

\displaystyle T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}=T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}+T^{2}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}},

where
T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
and
T^{2}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
are defined by

\displaystyle T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\triangleq

\displaystyle-\frac{9}{4\alpha}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})D_{\theta}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\frac{3}{2}(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2})L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma},

\displaystyle T^{2}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\triangleq

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\alpha}{2}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}+\frac{U(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))}{\sin(2\theta)}\right)D_{\theta}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}+V(\Phi-G^{\ast}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\sin(2\theta))\alpha D_{z}F^{\ast}_{\gamma}.

Under the assumptions (
4.16
) and (
4.18
), we can verify that both
T^{2}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
and
T_{g}
satisfy the expected estimate, namely,

\displaystyle T^{2}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}),\quad T_{g}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

But for
T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
, we need to conduct a more detailed analysis.
Firstly, we should first ensure that
T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\big|_{t=0}=0
.
Indeed, we deduce from (
3.6
) and (
3.18
) that

\displaystyle\frac{T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}}{L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{4\alpha}\frac{D_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}+\frac{3}{2}\left(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2}\right)\frac{D_{z}\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}}{\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma}}

(4.29)

\displaystyle=-\frac{9}{4\alpha}\frac{D_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}+\frac{3}{2\gamma}\left(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2}\right)\left(-1+L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\right).

Choosing

\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}=(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}
, one gets, by (
4.22
) and (
4.29
), that

\displaystyle\frac{T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}}{L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}\bigg|_{z=0}=-\frac{9}{4\alpha}\frac{D_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}+\frac{3}{2\gamma}\left(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2}\right)=0.

(4.30)

Furthermore, combining (
4.29
) and (
4.30
), then recalling that
\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}=(K(\theta))^{\frac{\alpha}{3\gamma}}
, we can rewrite
T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}
as

\displaystyle T^{1}_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}

\displaystyle=L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma}\left(-\frac{9}{4\alpha}\frac{D_{\theta}\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}{\Gamma_{\theta,\gamma}}+\frac{3}{2\gamma}\left(\cos(2\theta)-(\sin\theta)^{2}\right)\left(-1+L^{-1}_{z}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\gamma})\right)\right)

\displaystyle=-\frac{3}{\gamma}\left(\cos(2\theta)-\sin^{2}\theta\right)\frac{z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}{1+z^{\frac{1}{\gamma}}}L^{-1}_{z,K}(F^{\ast}_{\gamma})F^{\ast}_{\gamma}=\tilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

Finally, we conclude that

\displaystyle T_{F^{\ast}_{\gamma}}=\widetilde{o}(\Gamma^{\ast}_{\beta}).

(4.31)

(2) Analysis of
T_{g}

For
\mathcal{W}\in C((0,\infty)\times(0,\frac{\pi}{2}))
that would be determined later, we perform the decomposition of
T_{g}
below

\displaystyle T_{g}=\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}-\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g,

(4.32)

where the notations
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}
and
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g
are defined by

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}\triangleq\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}\left(\frac{U(\Phi)}{\sin(2\theta)}D_{\theta}(g\mathcal{W})+V(\Phi)\alpha D_{z}(g\mathcal{W})\right),\quad\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g\triangleq\left(\frac{U(\Phi)}{\sin(2\theta)}\frac{D_{\theta}\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}}+\alpha V(\Phi)\frac{D_{z}\mathcal{W}}{\mathcal{W}}\right)g.

We point out that
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{g\mathcal{W}}
is the null part, while
\frac{1}{\mathcal{W}}T_{\mathcal{W}}\cdot g
is the homogeneous part.
We aim to find the weight
\mathcal{W}
, such that the null part can inherit the structure from the divergence-free condition.
This enables us to treat
T_{g}
as the null perturbation of the homogeneous part.

(2.1) Analysis of the null part

We note that the null structure is crucial for establishing global well-posedness with critical regularity.
